## aqa-gcse-biology-p1: AQA GCSE Biology Paper 1 (8461) higher tier

*UK GCSE, one paper of a two-paper subject (AQA 8461).* Score **100**, planned as **exam**, due 2026-12-25, 24 s, 3 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning AQA GCSE Biology Paper 1 (8461) higher tier → Finding the syllabus → Researching this syllabus for the first time → Read 8 sources → Double-checking the topics → Double-check made 3 corrections → 14 topics from research, double-checked → Building your plan → 40 sessions to Thu 24 Dec → Fitting 40 pieces of work into your free time → 167 checks passed.
- Strategy: 14 topics revised and practised in order, 4 mixed reviews, 3 timed Paper 1s, light final day. Time is tight, so sessions are short.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 50: fewer than two spaced retrieval sessions; no light review on Thu 24 Dec; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 60 min
- Syllabus **AQA GCSE Biology (8461), Paper 1, Higher tier** from model (researched), library new v1 CHECKED; identity AQA / GCSE / Biology / 8461 / Paper 1
- Papers: Paper 1 105 min, 100 marks
- Sources: https://aqa.org.uk/subjects/biology/gcse/biology-8461/specification/specification-at-a-glance?utm_source=presse&utm_medium=link&utm_campaign=partner-werden-2024, https://cognito.org/specifications/gcse/biology/aqa, https://learndirect.com/category/gcse-courses/resources/gcse-biology-paper-1-topics, http://gordons.school/_site/data/files/pages/curriculum/F33FA35F3E3A0DC1507EED9A09B1C6AC.pdf, https://revisiongenie.com/subject/gcse/biology/aqa/specification-overview, https://cchs.co.uk/wp-content/uploads/2023/12/Biology-KS4-Curriculum.pdf

Topics:

1. **Cell structure and microscopy** w3 d2: Label the structures of animal, plant and bacterial cells and state their functions; Compare eukaryotic and prokaryotic cells and give examples of each; Explain how cell specialisation and differentiation suit a cell's function; Calculate magnification, image size and real size in µm and mm; Describe how to use a light microscope to observe and draw cells (RP1); Compare light and electron microscopes in resolution and magnification; Explain order of magnitude and standard form for cell sizes; Describe how sperm, nerve and muscle cells are adapted
2. **Cell division and stem cells** w2 d2: Describe the stages of the cell cycle including interphase and mitosis; Explain why mitosis produces genetically identical diploid cells; Describe how the cell cycle is used in growth and repair; State the sources of embryonic and adult stem cells, and plant meristems; Evaluate the uses and risks of stem cells in medicine and therapy; Calculate the number of cells after repeated mitotic division
3. **Transport in cells** w3 d2: Explain diffusion in terms of particle concentration and temperature; Describe factors affecting the rate of diffusion and give examples; Explain osmosis in terms of water potential across a partially permeable membrane; Explain the effects of osmosis on animal and plant cells; Investigate osmosis using plant tissue masses (RP3); Calculate percentage change in mass and identify the isotonic point; Describe active transport and give examples such as root hair cells and the gut; Compare diffusion, osmosis and active transport
4. **Principles of organisation and enzymes** w3 d2: Describe the levels of organisation from cells to organ systems; Explain how digestive enzymes break down carbohydrates, proteins and lipids; State where amylase, protease and lipase are produced and act; Explain the effect of temperature on enzyme activity and denaturation; Explain the effect of pH on enzyme activity and the optimum pH; Use the lock-and-key model to explain enzyme specificity; Describe how to test foods for sugars, starch, protein and lipids (RP4); Investigate the effect of pH on amylase activity (RP5)
5. **The circulatory system** w3 d2: Label the heart: atria, ventricles, valves, aorta and vena cava; Describe the double circulatory system and its advantages; Explain how the heart rate is controlled by the pacemaker; Describe arteries, veins and capillaries and relate structure to function; Describe the composition of blood and the function of each component; Explain how stents, statins and artificial valves treat heart disease; Calculate cardiac output from heart rate and stroke volume
6. **Health and disease** w2 d2: Define health and describe causes of ill health; Describe risk factors for non-communicable disease, e.g. cancer and CHD; Explain how lifestyle and genetic factors interact in disease; Describe how benign and malignant tumours form; Interpret data on disease risk factors and correlations
7. **Plant organs and transport** w2 d2: Describe the tissues of a leaf and their functions; Explain how the root hair cell and xylem are adapted for water uptake; Describe transpiration and factors affecting its rate; Explain translocation in the phloem and the role of sieve tubes; Label the structures of a leaf, stem and root from a diagram
8. **Communicable diseases** w3 d2: State that communicable diseases are spread by pathogens: bacteria, viruses, fungi, protists; Describe measles, HIV and tobacco mosaic virus symptoms and spread; Describe salmonella and gonorrhoea and how their spread is controlled; Describe rose black spot and malaria, including the vector; Explain how pathogens cause damage and how diseases are transmitted; Suggest ways to reduce the spread of disease
9. **Human defence and vaccination** w3 d2: Describe the non-specific defences: skin, mucus, cilia, stomach acid; Explain the role of white blood cells in phagocytosis and antibody production; Explain how vaccination gives immunity and prevents epidemics; Describe the immune response to a first and second infection; Evaluate the use of vaccination and herd immunity, including risks
10. **Drugs, antibiotics and drug development** w2 d2: Describe the discovery and development of new drugs from plants; Explain how antibiotics work and why resistance develops; Explain why antibiotics do not work on viruses and painkillers only treat symptoms; Describe the stages of clinical trials including placebo and double-blind; Evaluate benefits and risks of antibiotic use and drug testing
11. **Monoclonal antibodies (Higher)** w1 d3: Describe how monoclonal antibodies are produced from a single cell; Explain how monoclonal antibodies are used in pregnancy tests; Describe their use in cancer treatment and diagnosis; Evaluate the advantages and problems of monoclonal antibody therapy
12. **Plant disease and defences** w1 d2: Describe plant diseases and their detection methods; Identify common plant diseases from symptoms and data; Describe physical, chemical and mechanical plant defences; Explain plant defences and adaptations such as thorns and poisons
13. **Photosynthesis and limiting factors** w3 d2: Write the word and balanced equation for photosynthesis; Explain how light, temperature and CO2 limit the rate of photosynthesis; Interpret graphs of rate against light intensity and other factors; Explain how glucose is used: respiration, starch, cellulose, amino acids; Investigate the effect of light intensity on pondweed (RP6); Explain how photosynthesis was investigated historically; Use limiting factor graphs to predict rate changes
14. **Aerobic and anaerobic respiration** w3 d2: Write the equation for aerobic respiration and state energy uses; Compare aerobic and anaerobic respiration in muscles and yeast; Explain the effects of exercise on the body and response to oxygen debt; Describe metabolism and the role of the liver in lactic acid breakdown; Interpret data on respiration and exercise response

## ib-maths-aa-hl-course: IB Mathematics: Analysis and Approaches HL, whole course to the May exams

*IB Diploma, a whole two-year course to the final exams, several papers.* Score **96**, planned as **course**, due 2027-05-04, 28.7 s, 3 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning IB Mathematics: Analysis and Approaches HL, whole course to → Mapping the course → Researching this syllabus for the first time → Read 8 sources → Double-checking the topics → Double-check made 7 corrections → 19 topics from research, double-checked → Building your plan → 123 sessions to Mon 3 May → Fitting 123 pieces of work into your free time → 192 checks passed.
- ✗ **leaves out /internal assessment|exploration/**: A topic title matches /internal assessment|exploration/: Internal assessment: Mathematical exploration
- Strategy: 19 topics in order, 14 reviews, 3 checkpoint tests, 8 timed papers.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 50: fewer than two spaced retrieval sessions; no light review on Mon 3 May; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 60 min
- Syllabus **IB Mathematics: Analysis and Approaches HL** from model (researched), library new v1 CHECKED; identity International Baccalaureate / IB Diploma / Mathematics: Analysis and Approaches / HL
- Papers: Paper 1 120 min, 110 marks; Paper 2 120 min, 110 marks; Paper 3 60 min, 55 marks
- Sources: https://resources.ggs.vic.edu.au/curriculum/ib-diploma/group-5-mathematics, https://acsindep.moe.edu.sg/files/IB%20Evening/group_5_mathematics_course_outline_2024.pdf, https://ibo.org/fr/university-admission/latest-curriculum-updates/dp-mathematics-analysis-and-approaches-updates, https://mescairo.com/PDF/2026-2027/IBDP%20Options%20Booklet%2026-28.pdf, https://feg-bonn.de/wp-content/uploads/2024/09/05-Mathematics.pdf, https://nordangliaeducation.com/bis-hanoi/-/media/bis-hanoi/documents-and-pdfs/2023-24-sy/curriculum-booklet/ib-curriculum-handbook-class-of-2026.pdf?rev=1a61245e003e459d97daba2a684a93ee&hash=AA6B3A767EB125838879FAA338441194

Topics:

1. **Sequences and series** w2 d2: Use arithmetic and geometric sequences, nth term and sum formulas; Use sigma notation and apply the binomial theorem; Find sums of finite arithmetic and geometric series; Solve financial problems: compound interest, depreciation, annuities; Use counting principles, permutations and combinations; Solve problems on the binomial expansion with rational indices
2. **Number and algebra** w2 d2: Apply laws of exponents with rational exponents; Solve exponential equations using logarithms; Use the laws of logarithms, including change of base; Solve logarithmic and exponential inequalities; Sketch and analyse exponential and logarithmic graphs; Solve equations of the form a^x = b using logarithms
3. **Proof and mathematical induction** w2 d3: Use direct proof and proof by contradiction; Use proof by counterexample; Prove statements by mathematical induction; Apply proof to divisibility and inequality statements; Distinguish when each method is appropriate; Construct rigorous arguments with clear reasoning
4. **Permutations** w2 d2: Counting principles and permutations and combinations; Binomial coefficients and their relationship to combinations; Decompose rational expressions into partial fractions; Use partial fractions in integration and series work
5. **Complex numbers** w3 d3: Perform arithmetic with complex numbers in Cartesian form; Find modulus, argument, conjugate and reciprocal; Use polar (trigonometric) and exponential forms; Multiply and divide complex numbers in polar form; Use De Moivre's theorem to find roots and powers; Solve polynomial equations with real coefficients, including conjugate roots; Represent complex numbers on an Argand diagram
6. **Systems of linear equations** w2 d2: Solve 3x3 systems using row reduction / elimination; Identify unique, infinite and no-solution cases; Use matrices or technology to solve systems; Interpret geometric meaning of solutions for lines and planes; Apply partial fractions to rational expressions; Solve linear equations with polynomial identity methods
7. **Functions and graphs** w3 d2: Find domain, range, inverse and composite functions; Use the factor and remainder theorems; Find sums and products of polynomial roots; Sketch rational functions with asymptotes and intercepts; Identify odd, even and self-inverse functions; Use the modulus function and solve inequalities; Transform graphs: translations, stretches, reflections; Solve equations graphically and with technology
8. **Functions** w2 d2: Use the discriminant to determine the nature of roots; Complete the square and find vertex, axis of symmetry; Solve quadratic inequalities; Find the roots of polynomials, including multiplicity; Solve equations reducible to quadratic form
9. **Triangle trigonometry** w2 d2: Use the sine and cosine rules and area formula; Solve problems using bearings and 3D figures; Use reciprocal trigonometric ratios (sec, cosec, cot); Use Pythagorean and quotient identities; Solve trigonometric equations with compound angles; Apply inverse trigonometric functions and their domains
10. **Circular functions and identities** w3 d3: Convert between degrees and radians and use arc length and sector area; Use the unit circle and reference angles; Apply compound and double angle identities; Use symmetry to simplify and solve trigonometric expressions; Sketch trigonometric functions and identify transformations; Solve trigonometric equations in given intervals
11. **Further trigonometry** w2 d2: Define and use inverse trigonometric functions, including their domains and ranges; Solve equations using inverse trigonometric functions; Apply the double angle identity for tangent; Apply compound angle identities to solve equations
12. **Vectors** w3 d3: Use vector notation, addition, subtraction and scalar multiplication; Find magnitude and unit vectors in 2D and 3D; Compute scalar (dot) product and cross product; Find angles between vectors; Solve equations of lines and planes; Find intersections and shortest distances involving lines and planes
13. **Statistics and probability** w3 d2: Use descriptive statistics: mean, median, quartiles, standard deviation, IQR; Interpret box plots, histograms and cumulative frequency; Calculate probabilities using sample spaces, Venn diagrams and tree diagrams; Apply conditional probability and independent events; Use Bayes' theorem; Solve problems with mutually exclusive and exhaustive events
14. **Probability distributions** w3 d2: Use discrete random variables and probability mass functions; Find expected value, variance and standard deviation; Work with the binomial distribution and its conditions; Use the normal distribution and standardisation; Find probabilities using normal distribution and inverse normal; Use probability density functions and expectation algebra
15. **Limits and differential calculus** w3 d3: Understand informal limits and convergence; Differentiate from first principles; Differentiate polynomial, exponential, logarithmic and trigonometric functions; Apply product, quotient and chain rules; Differentiate inverse and reciprocal trigonometric functions; Use implicit differentiation; Apply L'Hopital's rule; Find tangent and normal equations
16. **Applications of differential calculus** w3 d3: Analyse increasing/decreasing behaviour using first derivative; Find stationary points and classify them; Use the second derivative, concavity and points of inflection; Solve optimisation problems; Solve kinematics problems with displacement, velocity and acceleration
17. **Integration** w3 d3: Find antiderivatives of standard functions; Compute definite and indefinite integrals; Use definite integrals for areas and volumes of revolution; Apply integration by substitution; Apply integration by parts; Apply partial fractions to integration
18. **Calculus** w3 d3: Solve first-order differential equations by separation of variables; Use an integrating factor for linear first-order equations; Apply Euler's method to approximate solutions; Use Maclaurin series for standard functions; Use Taylor series and error bounds; Model real contexts with differential equations
19. **Internal assessment: Mathematical exploration** w3 d3: Choose a topic with sufficient mathematical content for HL; Formulate a clear research question; Develop the mathematics coherently and rigorously; Justify methods and evaluate results; Include correct notation, graphs and diagrams; Reflect on limitations and possible extensions; Present a well-structured written report

## jee-main-physics: JEE Main Physics preparation

*Indian national entrance exam (JEE Main), heavy daily load, weak topics.* Score **100**, planned as **exam**, due 2027-02-11, 27.7 s, 4 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning JEE Main Physics preparation → Finding the syllabus → Researching this syllabus for the first time → Read 8 sources → Double-checking the topics → Checking 2 more official topics → Double-check made 13 corrections → 24 topics from research, double-checked → Building your plan → 121 sessions to Wed 10 Feb → Fitting 121 pieces of work into your free time → 248 checks passed.
- Strategy: 24 topics revised and practised in order, 8 mixed reviews, 6 timed Physics sections, light final day.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 50: fewer than two spaced retrieval sessions; no light review on Wed 10 Feb; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 180 min
- Syllabus **JEE Main Physics** from model (researched), library new v1 CHECKED; identity National Testing Agency (NTA) / JEE Main / Physics
- Papers: Physics section 60 min, 100 marks
- Sources: https://sathee.iitk.ac.in/sathee-jee/article/engineering/jee_main_syllabus_2024_released_download_physics_chemistry_maths_new_pdf, https://manavrachna.edu.in/blog/jee-main-2026-session-1-exam-date-syllabus-pattern--what-comes-next, https://motion.ac.in/jee-main-syllabus, https://educationtimes.com/news-jee-main-2027-syllabus-released-at-jeemain-nta-nic-in-are-there-any-changes-download-pdf-check-subject-wise-units/articleshow/135188717.cms, https://education.sakshi.com/en/jee-main/education-news/jee-main-2027-syllabus-released-nta-paper-1-paper-2-topics-pdf-download-201727, https://pw.live/iit-jee/exams/jee-main-2026-physics-ke-in-topics-ko-na-padhen-yahan-dekhen-hataya-gaya-syllabus

Topics:

1. **Units and Measurements** w2 d1: Convert between SI units and use fundamental and derived units; Find dimensions of physical quantities and check dimensional consistency; Apply dimensional analysis to derive relations and convert units; Use significant figures and rounding correctly in results; Compute errors: absolute, relative, percentage, and in combined quantities; Use least count and vernier/screw gauge readings
2. **Kinematics** w2 d2: Distinguish frame of reference, distance, displacement, speed and velocity; Interpret position-time and velocity-time graphs; Solve uniformly accelerated motion with the three equations of motion; Handle relative velocity in one and two dimensions; Analyse projectile motion: range, maximum height, time of flight; Solve uniform circular motion: angular speed, centripetal acceleration
3. **Laws of Motion** w2 d2: Apply Newton's three laws and the concept of inertia and momentum; Use impulse-momentum theorem and conservation of linear momentum; Solve equilibrium of concurrent forces using free-body diagrams; Analyse static and kinetic friction, including rolling friction; Solve dynamics of uniform circular motion: level and banked roads; Handle connected bodies, pulleys and constraint relations
4. **Work, Energy and Power** w2 d2: Compute work done by constant and variable forces; Apply the work-energy theorem to find speeds and displacements; Use potential energy of a spring and conservation of mechanical energy; Distinguish conservative and non-conservative forces; Solve motion in a vertical circle including tension at key points; Analyse elastic and inelastic collisions in one and two dimensions; Relate power to force and velocity
5. **Rotational Motion** (weak) w3 d3: Locate the centre of mass of two-particle systems and rigid bodies; Compute torque, angular momentum and apply conservation of angular momentum; Calculate moment of inertia for standard bodies and use radius of gyration; Apply parallel and perpendicular axes theorems; Use equations of rotational motion for fixed-axis rotation; Apply conditions for equilibrium of rigid bodies; Compare linear and rotational quantities
6. **Gravitation** w2 d2: Apply Newton's universal law of gravitation; Find variation of g with altitude, depth and latitude; Use Kepler's laws of planetary motion; Compute gravitational potential and potential energy; Derive escape velocity; Solve orbital velocity, time period and energy of satellites
7. **Properties of Solids and Liquids** w3 d2: Use stress-strain relations, Hooke's law and elastic moduli; Compute pressure due to a fluid column and apply Pascal's law; Apply Stokes' law and terminal velocity; Distinguish streamline and turbulent flow, use critical velocity and Reynolds number; Apply Bernoulli's theorem and its applications; Solve capillary rise and surface tension problems; Handle thermal expansion, calorimetry and heat transfer
8. **Thermodynamics** w3 d2: State the zeroth and first laws of thermodynamics and apply them; Distinguish isothermal, adiabatic, isobaric and isochoric processes; Compute work done in thermodynamic processes and draw PV diagrams; Apply the second law and describe heat engines and refrigerators; Find efficiency and coefficient of performance; Use specific heats Cp, Cv and relate them to molar values
9. **Kinetic Theory of Gases** w2 d2: Relate pressure to molecular motion in kinetic theory; Use the ideal gas equation and degrees of freedom; Compute rms, average and most probable speeds; Apply the law of equipartition of energy to find specific heats; Solve mean free path problems
10. **Oscillations and Waves** w3 d3: Write SHM displacement, velocity and acceleration equations; Find time period of spring-mass and simple pendulum systems; Compute energy in SHM and analyse damped oscillations; Apply the wave equation and find speed, frequency and wavelength; Analyse superposition, stationary waves and beats; Solve problems on resonance, harmonics in strings and pipes; Apply the Doppler effect for moving source and observer
11. **Electrostatics** (weak) w3 d3: Apply Coulomb's law and find resultant field and force; Compute electric field of point, line, sheet and sphere of charge; Apply Gauss's law to symmetric charge distributions; Find electric potential and potential difference; Compute capacitance of parallel-plate and spherical capacitors; Solve series and parallel capacitor combinations and energy stored; Analyse dielectrics and their effect on capacitance
12. **Current Electricity** w3 d2: Apply Ohm's law, resistivity and series-parallel resistor combinations; Analyse Kirchhoff's laws in circuits; Use Wheatstone bridge and metre bridge conditions; Solve problems with EMF, internal resistance and potential divider; Compute power and energy in circuits and use potentiometer; Handle cells in series and parallel and their combinations
13. **Potentiometer** w2 d2: Potentiometer: principle and comparison of EMFs; Measurement of internal resistance; Metre bridge applications and electrical instruments
14. **Magnetism and Moving Charges** w3 d3: Compute magnetic force on a moving charge and use the concept of magnetic field; Find field of a current element using Biot-Savart law; Apply the force on a current-carrying conductor in a magnetic field; Analyse force between parallel currents and use Ampère's circuital law; Solve circular motion of charged particles in magnetic fields; Find torque on a current loop and use the moving coil galvanometer
15. **Magnetism and Matter** w2 d2: Distinguish magnetic field lines, magnetic dipoles and pole strength; Apply the bar magnet in uniform fields: torque and potential energy; Use Gauss's law for magnetism and earth's magnetic elements; Distinguish dia-, para- and ferromagnetic materials; Describe hysteresis, retentivity and coercivity
16. **Electromagnetic Waves** w2 d2: Describe the nature and characteristics of electromagnetic waves and their transverse character; Relate the amplitudes of electric and magnetic fields in an electromagnetic wave; State the electromagnetic spectrum and order its components by wavelength and frequency; Give common uses of the different parts of the electromagnetic spectrum
17. **Electromagnetic Induction** w2 d2: Faraday's and Lenz's laws, motional EMF, eddy currents; Self and mutual inductance; AC circuits with R, L and C: impedance, phase, resonance, power factor; LC oscillations and transformers
18. **Ray Optics and Optical Instruments** w3 d2: Apply reflection and refraction at plane and spherical surfaces; Use lens and mirror formulae and magnification; Analyse total internal reflection and critical angle; Solve problems with prisms and dispersion; Compute combinations of thin lenses and equivalent focal length; Handle simple microscope, compound microscope and telescope
19. **Wave Optics** w2 d3: Apply Young's double-slit interference: fringe width and intensity; Analyse single-slit diffraction and diffraction patterns; Use polarisation by reflection, Brewster's law and polarisers; Handle coherent sources and conditions for sustained interference; Apply resolving power and optical instruments
20. **Dual Nature of Matter and Radiation** w2 d2: Apply the photoelectric effect: Einstein's photoelectric equation and work function; Use particle nature of light and photon energy, momentum relations; Apply de Broglie wavelength of matter waves to electrons and other particles; Describe the Davisson-Germer experiment demonstrating wave nature of electrons
21. **Atoms** w2 d2: Apply Rutherford's scattering model and its predictions; Derive Bohr model radius, velocity and energy levels; Compute spectral series of hydrogen and energy transitions; Apply de Broglie's explanation of Bohr's quantisation
22. **Nuclei** w2 d2: Use atomic mass unit, binding energy and mass defect; Interpret the binding energy per nucleon curve; Apply radioactive decay law, half-life and mean life; AnalYse nuclear fission and fusion reactions; Handle nuclear forces and stability
23. **Semiconductor Devices** w2 d2: Distinguish intrinsic and extrinsic semiconductors and doping; Analyse p-n junction, depletion region and biasing; Apply diode characteristics and rectifier behaviour; Solve Zener diode as regulator and special purpose diodes (LED, photodiode, solar cell); Explain logic gates and truth tables
24. **Experimental Skills** w2 d2: Choose instruments and read vernier callipers and screw gauge; Describe common experiments: Ohm's law, metre bridge, potentiometer; Analyse errors in experiments and identify sources of error; Interpret simple laboratory graphs and results

## aws-saa-c03: AWS Certified Solutions Architect Associate (SAA-C03) exam

*Professional certification, short weekday sessions.* Score **97**, planned as **exam**, due 2026-12-18, 35.4 s, 4 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning AWS Certified Solutions Architect Associate (SAA-C03) exam → Finding the syllabus → Researching this syllabus for the first time → Read 8 sources → Double-checking the topics → Checking 2 more official topics → Double-check made 10 corrections → 22 topics from research, double-checked → Building your plan → 28 sessions to Thu 17 Dec → Fitting 28 pieces of work into your free time → 145 checks passed.
- ✗ **topic count**: 22 topics, expected 4–20
- Strategy: 22 topics revised and practised in order, 2 timed papers (SAA-C03 exam), light final day. Time is tight, so sessions are short.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 50: fewer than two spaced retrieval sessions; no light review on Thu 17 Dec; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 81: 4 sessions are much shorter than ideal.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 45 min
- Syllabus **AWS Certified Solutions Architect – Associate (SAA-C03)** from model (researched), library new v1 CHECKED; identity Amazon Web Services / Certification / Solutions Architect / SAA-C03
- Papers: SAA-C03 exam 130 min, 65 marks
- Sources: https://myportal.utt.edu.tt/ICS/icsfs/ff9a6162-7440-4379-80d6-35d04c2f6903.pdf?target=3eb5e58d-a054-46e6-98aa-ea1611962ed3, https://docs.aws.amazon.com/pdfs/aws-certification/latest/solutions-architect-associate-03/solutions-architect-associate-03.pdf, https://whizlabs.com/blog/saa-c03-syllabus, https://factualminds.com/certifications/aws-solutions-architect-associate, http://mp.weixin.qq.com/s?__biz=Mzk0MzQ5NTcwNA==&mid=2247497308&idx=1&sn=6f8d8b3a573593d019ebaef0bc29ac0b, https://pluralsight.com/resources/blog/cloud/AWS-saa-c03-exam

Topics:

1. **IAM, Organizations and access control** w3 d2: Write IAM policies and use roles for cross-account access; Apply permission boundaries and service control policies; Use IAM Identity Center for workforce SSO with external IdPs; Organise accounts with AWS Organizations OUs and Control Tower; Choose managed policies over inline for reusable permissions; Troubleshoot access denied using policy evaluation logic
2. **VPC network security and inspection** w3 d3: Configure security groups versus network ACLs correctly; Use VPC endpoints (gateway and interface) and AWS PrivateLink; Deploy AWS WAF managed and rate-based rules; Compare Shield Standard and Shield Advanced protection; Use Systems Manager Session Manager instead of a bastion host; Inspect traffic with VPC Flow Logs and Network Firewall
3. **Threat detection** w2 d2: GuardDuty findings for anomalous and malicious activity; Macie for S3 PII detection and classification; Security Hub for aggregating findings across accounts; When each service is the right answer in a scenario
4. **Encryption, KMS and secrets management** w3 d3: Design encryption at rest with KMS customer-managed keys; Use multi-region keys and key rotation for cross-region data; Encrypt S3 objects and EBS volumes with KMS and default keys; Terminate TLS with ACM certificates on ALB, NLB and CloudFront; Rotate database credentials with Secrets Manager versus Parameter Store; Detect sensitive data and threats with Macie, GuardDuty and Security Hub
5. **Secrets management and bastion alternatives** w2 d2: Secrets Manager rotation patterns versus Parameter Store SecureString; When each secrets service is the right design choice; Bastion host versus Systems Manager Session Manager (Session Manager wins)
6. **Multi-tier and loosely coupled compute** w3 d2: Design multi-tier architectures with public and private subnets; Place compute on EC2, containers (ECS, EKS) or Lambda appropriately; Scale fleets with Auto Scaling groups and target tracking; Serve traffic with ALB, NLB and Gateway Load Balancer; Decouple layers with SQS queues and SNS topics; Route events with EventBridge and apply microservice principles
7. **High availability and multi-AZ design** w3 d2: Choose Multi-AZ over single-instance designs for availability; Use Route 53 failover, latency and geolocation routing with health checks; Compare RDS Multi-AZ with read replicas; Design stateless tiers that survive instance failure; Use Elastic Beanstalk environments for managed deployments; Plan bastion-free, highly available access patterns
8. **Backup, DR and cross-region resilience** w2 d3: Distinguish backup-restore, pilot light, warm standby and active-active; Set RPO and RTO targets and pick the matching DR pattern; Replicate S3 with CRR/SRR and Replication Time Control; Use DynamoDB global tables, PITR and on-demand backups; Protect data with AWS Backup across services; Use Aurora Global Database for cross-region recovery
9. **Queuing, messaging and decoupling** w2 d3: Tune SQS visibility timeout and retry policies; Configure dead-letter queues to capture failed messages; Choose SQS standard versus FIFO for ordering needs; Fan out with SNS to multiple subscribers; Orchestrate retries and compensation with Step Functions; Apply idempotency to make retried processing safe
10. **Storage performance** w2 d3: Select EBS volume types (gp3, io2 Block Express, st1, sc1); Tune EFS performance and throughput modes; Choose between FSx for Lustre, Windows and NetApp ONTAP; Size S3 throughput for high request rates; Match Instance Store versus EBS to workload needs; Configure RAID and multiple volumes for IOPS targets
11. **Caching and content delivery** w2 d2: Design CloudFront distributions with cache behaviours and TTLs; Configure CloudFront origin failover and signed URLs; Choose Lambda@Edge versus CloudFront Functions; Use Global Accelerator for non-HTTP anycast traffic; Compare ElastiCache with DAX for database caching; Place caching layers to reduce latency and load
12. **In-memory and DAX caching for databases** w2 d2: ElastiCache (Valkey/Redis OSS) for read-heavy and session data; DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) for microsecond DynamoDB reads; Trade-offs between ElastiCache, DAX and on-demand caching; Cache invalidation and TTL strategies
13. **Database performance and scaling** w2 d3: Scale reads with RDS and Aurora read replicas (5 versus 15); Use Aurora serverless and Global Database for variable or global load; Pick DynamoDB partition keys and indexes for performance; Use DynamoDB on-demand versus provisioned capacity; Optimise RDS instance classes and storage for workload; Apply read/write splitting in the application tier
14. **Aurora DSQL and distributed database options** w2 d2: Aurora DSQL for distributed PostgreSQL at scale; Positioning against Aurora Global Database and DynamoDB global tables; When a distributed SQL database is the right design choice
15. **Serverless and API performance** w2 d2: Configure Lambda provisioned and reserved concurrency; Use Lambda SnapStart to cut cold starts; Throttle and cache at API Gateway with usage plans; Create and manage REST and HTTP APIs; Choose synchronous versus asynchronous invocation patterns; Design responsive serverless event-driven workflows
16. **EC2 and compute cost optimisation** w2 d2: Compare On-Demand, Savings Plans and Reserved Instances; Apply Spot instances to interruption-tolerant workloads; Choose Compute Savings Plans for Lambda and Fargate coverage; Right-size instances using Compute Optimizer; Match instance families to workload type; Use Auto Scaling schedules to cut idle capacity
17. **Cost Optimization Hub and right-sizing tooling** w2 d2: Cost Optimization Hub for consolidated recommendations; Compute Optimizer for EC2, ASG, EBS and Lambda right-sizing; Acting on rightsizing and idle-resource findings
18. **Storage cost optimisation** w2 d2: Select S3 storage classes including Intelligent-Tiering and Glacier tiers; Write S3 lifecycle rules to transition and expire objects; Use S3 Storage Lens for visibility and recommendations; Choose EBS types and snapshots economically; Move cold data to archival storage for databases and file systems; Deduplicate and compress backup data with AWS Backup
19. **Networking and data transfer cost** w2 d3: Use VPC endpoints to avoid NAT Gateway data charges; Weigh S3 Transfer Acceleration against its cost; Serve outbound traffic through CloudFront; Place resources in the right region and AZ for cost; Understand cross-AZ and internet egress pricing; Choose single-NAT versus per-AZ NAT designs for cost
20. **Cost governance and monitoring** w1 d1: Set AWS Budgets alerts and Cost Anomaly Detection; Use Cost Explorer and Cost and Usage Reports to analyse spend; Design pay-per-request versus provisioned serverless models; Apply tagging strategies for cost allocation; Use Savings Plans recommendations to cut recurring spend; Recognise managed services that remove operational cost
21. **Generative AI cost levers (Bedrock)** w2 d2: Model selection to control per-token cost; Prompt Caching to reduce repeated input cost; Provisioned Throughput versus on-demand for steady workloads; Batch Inference for cost-efficient bulk jobs
22. **Event-driven orchestration with Step Functions** w2 d2: Step Functions for orchestrating retries and compensating transactions; EventBridge buses for routing events between services; Choosing Step Functions versus SQS/SNS for workflow coordination; Decoupling producers and consumers in event-driven designs

| 2026-11-18 | Secrets management revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-18 08:50–20m | Revise Secrets management and bastion alternatives and test yourself on it in one session. · Secrets Manager rotation patterns versus Parameter Store SecureString; When each secrets service is the right design choice; Bastion host versus Systems Manager Session Manager (Session Manager wins) |
| 2026-11-18 | Multi-tier and loosely coupled compute revision and practice | revise | 45 | 2026-11-18 09:15–25m, 2026-11-19 08:50–20m | Revise Multi-tier and loosely coupled compute and test yourself on it in one session. · Design multi-tier architectures with public and private subnets; Place compute on EC2, containers (ECS, EKS) or Lambda appropriately; Scale fleets with Auto Scaling groups and target tracking; Serve traffic with ALB, NLB and Gateway Load Balancer; Decouple layers with SQS queues and SNS topics; Route events with EventBridge and apply microservice principles |
| 2026-11-19 | High availability and multi-AZ design revision and practice | revise | 45 | 2026-11-19 09:15–25m, 2026-11-20 08:50–20m | Revise High availability and multi-AZ design and test yourself on it in one session. · Choose Multi-AZ over single-instance designs for availability; Use Route 53 failover, latency and geolocation routing with health checks; Compare RDS Multi-AZ with read replicas; Design stateless tiers that survive instance failure; Use Elastic Beanstalk environments for managed deployments; Plan bastion-free, highly available access patterns |
| 2026-11-20 | Backup, DR and cross-region resilience revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-20 09:15–20m | Revise Backup, DR and cross-region resilience and test yourself on it in one session. · Distinguish backup-restore, pilot light, warm standby and active-active; Set RPO and RTO targets and pick the matching DR pattern; Replicate S3 with CRR/SRR and Replication Time Control; Use DynamoDB global tables, PITR and on-demand backups; Protect data with AWS Backup across services; Use Aurora Global Database for cross-region recovery |
| 2026-11-21 | Queuing, messaging and decoupling revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-21 08:50–20m | Revise Queuing, messaging and decoupling and test yourself on it in one session. · Tune SQS visibility timeout and retry policies; Configure dead-letter queues to capture failed messages; Choose SQS standard versus FIFO for ordering needs; Fan out with SNS to multiple subscribers; Orchestrate retries and compensation with Step Functions; Apply idempotency to make retried processing safe |
| 2026-11-23 | Storage performance revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-23 08:50–20m | Revise Storage performance and test yourself on it in one session. · Select EBS volume types (gp3, io2 Block Express, st1, sc1); Tune EFS performance and throughput modes; Choose between FSx for Lustre, Windows and NetApp ONTAP; Size S3 throughput for high request rates; Match Instance Store versus EBS to workload needs; Configure RAID and multiple volumes for IOPS targets |
| week 2026-11-23:20m | Aurora DSQL and distributed database revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-23:20m | Revise Aurora DSQL and distributed database options and test yourself on it in one session. · Aurora DSQL for distributed PostgreSQL at scale; Positioning against Aurora Global Database and DynamoDB global tables; When a distributed SQL database is the right design choice |
| week 2026-11-23:20m | Serverless and API performance revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-23:20m | Revise Serverless and API performance and test yourself on it in one session. · Configure Lambda provisioned and reserved concurrency; Use Lambda SnapStart to cut cold starts; Throttle and cache at API Gateway with usage plans; Create and manage REST and HTTP APIs; Choose synchronous versus asynchronous invocation patterns; Design responsive serverless event-driven workflows |
| 2026-11-24 | Caching and content delivery revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-24 08:50–20m | Revise Caching and content delivery and test yourself on it in one session. · Design CloudFront distributions with cache behaviours and TTLs; Configure CloudFront origin failover and signed URLs; Choose Lambda@Edge versus CloudFront Functions; Use Global Accelerator for non-HTTP anycast traffic; Compare ElastiCache with DAX for database caching; Place caching layers to reduce latency and load |
| 2026-11-25 | In-memory and DAX caching revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-25 08:50–20m | Revise In-memory and DAX caching for databases and test yourself on it in one session. · ElastiCache (Valkey/Redis OSS) for read-heavy and session data; DynamoDB Accelerator (DAX) for microsecond DynamoDB reads; Trade-offs between ElastiCache, DAX and on-demand caching; Cache invalidation and TTL strategies |
| 2026-11-26 | Database performance and scaling revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-26 08:50–20m | Revise Database performance and scaling and test yourself on it in one session. · Scale reads with RDS and Aurora read replicas (5 versus 15); Use Aurora serverless and Global Database for variable or global load; Pick DynamoDB partition keys and indexes for performance; Use DynamoDB on-demand versus provisioned capacity; Optimise RDS instance classes and storage for workload; Apply read/write splitting in the application tier |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | EC2 and compute cost optimisation revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise EC2 and compute cost optimisation and test yourself on it in one session. · Compare On-Demand, Savings Plans and Reserved Instances; Apply Spot instances to interruption-tolerant workloads; Choose Compute Savings Plans for Lambda and Fargate coverage; Right-size instances using Compute Optimizer; Match instance families to workload type; Use Auto Scaling schedules to cut idle capacity |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | Cost Optimization Hub revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise Cost Optimization Hub and right-sizing tooling and test yourself on it in one session. · Cost Optimization Hub for consolidated recommendations; Compute Optimizer for EC2, ASG, EBS and Lambda right-sizing; Acting on rightsizing and idle-resource findings |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | Storage cost optimisation revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise Storage cost optimisation and test yourself on it in one session. · Select S3 storage classes including Intelligent-Tiering and Glacier tiers; Write S3 lifecycle rules to transition and expire objects; Use S3 Storage Lens for visibility and recommendations; Choose EBS types and snapshots economically; Move cold data to archival storage for databases and file systems; Deduplicate and compress backup data with AWS Backup |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | Networking and data transfer cost revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise Networking and data transfer cost and test yourself on it in one session. · Use VPC endpoints to avoid NAT Gateway data charges; Weigh S3 Transfer Acceleration against its cost; Serve outbound traffic through CloudFront; Place resources in the right region and AZ for cost; Understand cross-AZ and internet egress pricing; Choose single-NAT versus per-AZ NAT designs for cost |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | Cost governance and monitoring revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise Cost governance and monitoring and test yourself on it in one session. · Set AWS Budgets alerts and Cost Anomaly Detection; Use Cost Explorer and Cost and Usage Reports to analyse spend; Design pay-per-request versus provisioned serverless models; Apply tagging strategies for cost allocation; Use Savings Plans recommendations to cut recurring spend; Recognise managed services that remove operational cost |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | Generative AI cost levers (Bedrock) revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise Generative AI cost levers (Bedrock) and test yourself on it in one session. · Model selection to control per-token cost; Prompt Caching to reduce repeated input cost; Provisioned Throughput versus on-demand for steady workloads; Batch Inference for cost-efficient bulk jobs |
| week 2026-11-30:20m | Event-driven orchestration revision and practice | revise | 20 | 2026-11-30:20m | Revise Event-driven orchestration with Step Functions and test yourself on it in one session. · Step Functions for orchestrating retries and compensating transactions; EventBridge buses for routing events between services; Choosing Step Functions versus SQS/SNS for workflow coordination; Decoupling producers and consumers in event-driven designs |

## usmle-step1: USMLE Step 1 dedicated study

*Medical licensing exam, a very large syllabus over four months at six hours a day.* Score **97**, planned as **exam**, due 2027-03-13, 27.8 s, 3 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning USMLE Step 1 dedicated study → Working out the plan → Researching this syllabus for the first time → Read 8 sources → Double-checking the topics → Double-check made 11 corrections → 19 topics from research, double-checked → Building your plan → 174 sessions to Fri 12 Mar → Fitting 174 pieces of work into your free time → 299 checks passed.
- ✗ **no duplicate topics**: Duplicates: blood & lymphoreticular/immune systems, respiratory & renal/urinary systems, reproductive & endocrine systems
- Strategy: 19 topics revised and practised in order, 6 mixed reviews, 8 timed Step 1s, light final day.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 50: fewer than two spaced retrieval sessions; no light review on Fri 12 Mar; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 240 min
- Syllabus **USMLE Step 1** from model (researched), library new v1 CHECKED; identity USMLE / USMLE Step 1 / Medicine
- Papers: Step 1 480 min, 280 marks
- Sources: https://meded.ucsf.edu/sites/meded.ucsf.edu/files/inline-files/content_step1.pdf, https://amboss.com/us/knowledge/usmle-content-outline, https://usmle.org/exam-resources/step-1-materials/step-1-content-outline-and-specifications?trk=article-ssr-frontend-pulse_little-text-block, https://usmlestrike.com/ultimate-guide-to-the-usmle-exam-syllabus, https://scribd.com/document/1057777236/USMLE-Content-Outline-0, https://dedicatedprep.com/usmle-step-1-content-outline-explained

Topics:

1. **Human Development** w1 d2: Describe normal growth, development milestones and screening in infancy and childhood; Outline pubertal changes and adolescent cognitive and psychosocial development; Identify normal age-related findings and care in adulthood and older adulthood; Apply routine preventive care, vaccinations and safety counselling by age group; Recognise normal variation such as constitutional delay of growth
2. **Biostatistics & Epidemiology/Population Health** w2 d2: Interpret sensitivity, specificity, predictive values and likelihood ratios; Calculate and interpret relative risk, odds ratio, attributable risk and NNT; Recognise study designs and their biases, including selection and recall bias; Interpret confidence intervals, p-values and statistical power; Apply screening principles and measures of population health; Distinguish incidence from prevalence and crude from adjusted rates
3. **Behavioral Health & Nervous Systems/Special** w3 d3: Classify psychotic, mood, anxiety and personality disorders by criteria; Identify neurotransmitters and drug mechanisms relevant to psychiatric illness; Localise lesions by pattern of motor, sensory and cranial nerve deficits; Recognise stroke syndromes, demyelinating disease and CNS tumours; Describe dementia, delirium and substance use disorders; Outline embryology and vascular anatomy of the brain and spinal cord; Recognise disorders of special senses: vision, hearing, balance, smell and taste
4. **Blood & Lymphoreticular/Immune Systems** w3 d3: Interpret complete blood count, smear and iron studies in anaemia; Classify haemoglobinopathies, thalassaemias and haemolytic anaemias; Describe platelet disorders and coagulation pathways, including haemophilia and von Willebrand disease; Recognise leukaemias, lymphomas and myeloproliferative neoplasms; Outline transfusion reactions and blood product use; Identify infections and malignancies of lymphoid tissue and spleen; Describe anticoagulant and antiplatelet pharmacology
5. **Blood & Lymphoreticular/Immune Systems** w2 d3: Distinguish humoral, cell-mediated, complement and phagocyte immunodeficiencies; Recognise HIV infection, AIDS complications and immune reconstitution syndrome; Classify hypersensitivity reactions types I to IV with examples; Describe transplant rejection and graft-versus-host disease; Explain immunomodulating drug mechanisms and adverse effects; Outline vaccine types, indications and adverse effects
6. **Musculoskeletal, skin and subcutaneous tissue** w3 d2: Distinguish inflammatory arthritides and crystal arthropathies; Recognise bone disorders: osteoporosis, osteomalacia, Paget disease and fractures; Localise muscle and nerve lesions causing weakness; Classify primary skin lesions and describe common dermatitides; Identify skin infections, blistering diseases and cutaneous malignancies; Recognise soft tissue tumours and inherited connective tissue disorders; Describe antibiotics, steroids and immunosuppressants used in these conditions
7. **Cardiovascular system** w3 d3: Relate cardiac embryology and congenital lesions to presentation; Interpret murmurs, jugular venous pulse and ECG findings; Describe ischaemic heart disease and myocardial infarction; Outline cardiomyopathies, valvular disease and pericardial disease; Recognise arrhythmias and conduction disorders by mechanism; Explain heart failure and shock pathophysiology and treatment; Describe lipid-lowering, antihypertensive and antiarrhythmic drugs; Identify vasculitides and vascular disease
8. **Respiratory & Renal/Urinary Systems** w3 d2: Interpret pulmonary function tests and blood gases; Distinguish obstructive and restrictive lung diseases; Recognise pneumonia, tuberculosis and lung abscess; Describe pulmonary embolism, hypertension and adult respiratory distress syndrome; Identify lung carcinoma subtypes and paraneoplastic syndromes; Outline sleep apnoea and pleural diseases; Describe bronchodilators, corticosteroids and other respiratory drugs
9. **Respiratory & Renal/Urinary Systems** w3 d3: Apply renal physiology: glomerular filtration, clearance and acid-base handling; Classify nephrotic and nephritic syndromes by histology and presentation; Recognise acute kidney injury, chronic kidney disease and electrolyte disorders; Describe urinary tract infections, stones and obstruction; Identify renal tumours and congenital urinary anomalies; Explain diuretics, acid-base therapies and nephrotoxic drugs
10. **Gastrointestinal system** w3 d2: Localise gastrointestinal disease by symptoms and anatomy; Describe oesophageal and gastric disorders, including peptic ulcer disease and reflux; Recognise inflammatory bowel disease, malabsorption and coeliac disease; Outline liver disease: hepatitis, cirrhosis, portal hypertension and liver failure; Identify biliary and pancreatic disorders; Recognise gastrointestinal bleeding and tumours; Explain proton pump inhibitors, antiemetics and laxatives
11. **Reproductive & Endocrine Systems** w3 d3: Outline gonadal embryology and sexual differentiation disorders; Describe menstrual cycle physiology and menstrual disorders; Recognise pregnancy complications, including ectopic pregnancy and pre-eclampsia; Identify infections and neoplasms of the female and male reproductive tracts; Describe contraception and infertility causes; Explain hormonal therapies and drugs affecting uterine tone
12. **Reproductive & Endocrine Systems** w3 d3: Interpret thyroid, adrenal and pituitary function tests; Distinguish hyperthyroidism, hypothyroidism and thyroid nodules; Recognise diabetes mellitus, its complications and acute presentations; Describe adrenal disorders, including Cushing syndrome and Addison disease; Identify pituitary adenomas and their mass effects; Explain insulin, oral hypoglycaemics and hormone replacement; Recognise parathyroid and calcium metabolism disorders
13. **Multisystem Processes & Disorders** w3 d3: Describe nutritional deficiencies, obesity and vitamin toxicities; Explain sepsis, systemic inflammatory response and shock; Recognise inherited metabolic and lysosomal storage diseases; Outline oncogenesis, tumour markers and paraneoplastic syndromes; Describe immune-mediated multisystem diseases and amyloidosis; Apply principles of pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics; Recognise adverse drug reactions and toxicology syndromes; Describe effects of ageing and environmental injury on multiple systems
14. **Microbiology and infectious disease** w3 d3: Distinguish bacterial structure, virulence factors and culture characteristics; Describe viral replication and latency for major viral families; Recognise fungal and parasitic infections by presentation; Match organisms to classic clinical syndromes and laboratory findings; Identify notifiable infections and infection control principles; Explain antibiotic, antiviral and antifungal mechanisms and resistance
15. **Pharmacology principles** w2 d2: Apply absorption, distribution, metabolism and elimination to dosing; Relate mechanism of action to receptor types and signal transduction; Predict dose-response, potency, efficacy and therapeutic index; Recognise drug interactions via CYP450 induction and inhibition; Identify antidotes and management of overdose; Describe autonomic drug effects by receptor subtype
16. **Biochemistry and molecular biology** w3 d3: Describe glycolysis, gluconeogenesis, TCA cycle and oxidative phosphorylation; Identify enzyme deficiencies causing metabolic disease and their presentations; Outline lipid, amino acid and nucleotide metabolism disorders; Explain DNA replication, transcription, translation and mutations; Recognise chromosomal abnormalities and inheritance patterns; Describe cell cycle regulation, apoptosis and oncogenes; Relate vitamins and cofactors to deficiency syndromes
17. **Cell biology and histology** w2 d2: Identify tissue types and organ histology on microscopy; Describe cell organelles and cytoskeletal elements and their defects; Explain cell membrane transport, including channels and pumps; Recognise cell injury, necrosis and apoptosis morphologically; Outline inflammation, repair and wound healing; Describe extracellular matrix and connective tissue components
18. **Anatomy and embryology** w2 d2: Identify gross structures, foramina and relations in each body region; Relate nerve injuries to patterns of motor and sensory loss; Describe embryological development of each organ system; Recognise congenital anomalies from developmental failure; Interpret cross-sectional and imaging anatomy; Trace blood supply, drainage and lymphatic routes
19. **Social Sciences** w2 d2: Apply patient-centred interviewing and shared decision-making; Describe informed consent and confidentiality obligations; Recognise health disparities and social determinants of health; Explain quality improvement, patient safety and medical error reporting; Outline health care systems, insurance and access; Identify ethical and legal principles in clinical scenarios

## sapiens-reading: Read Sapiens: A Brief History of Humankind by Yuval Noah Harari

*Reading a non-fiction book in three weeks.* Score **97**, planned as **reading**, due 2026-12-04, 3.9 s, 1 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning Read Sapiens: A Brief History of Humankind by Yuval Noah Har → Finding the chapters → 5 parts from the AI → Building your plan → 16 sessions to Thu 3 Dec → Fitting 16 pieces of work into your free time → 159 checks passed.
- ✗ **topic count**: 5 topics, expected 12–25
- Strategy: 5 parts at a steady pace, with written recaps.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 100: No exams in view.; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 40 min
- Syllabus **Sapiens: A Brief History of Humankind** from model

Topics:

1. **The Cognitive Revolution** w2 d2: Trace how Homo sapiens came to dominate other human species; Explain the role of gossip and shared myths in large-group cooperation; Distinguish the Cognitive Revolution from later revolutions in the book's argument; Describe what archaeology and genetics say about early foragers
2. **The Agricultural Revolution** w3 d2: Explain the book's argument that farming was 'history's biggest fraud'; Describe the costs of agriculture for health, diet and workload; Follow how surplus food supported elites, states and written records; Weigh the counterarguments to the book's luxury-trap thesis
3. **The Unification of Humankind** w3 d2: Explain money as a system of shared trust; Trace how empires spread common cultures and norms; Describe religion's role in unifying large populations; Show how commerce, empire and religion wove separate worlds together
4. **The Scientific Revolution** w3 d3: Explain the discovery of ignorance as a driver of modern science; Link science, European imperialism and capitalism; Describe how credit and trust in the future fuelled growth; Assess the book's claim that progress and happiness are not the same
5. **The End of Homo Sapiens** w1 d2: Summarise the book's speculations on genetic engineering and cyborgs; Discuss the open questions it raises about the future of our species; Compare these projections with the book's own earlier arguments

## guitar-skill: Learn guitar from zero: open chords to playing my first full song

*A practical skill with no deadline, short frequent sessions.* Score **100**, planned as **skill**, due none, 6.9 s, 1 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning Learn guitar from zero: open chords to playing my first full → Mapping the skill → 8 stages from the AI → Building your plan → 41 sessions to Thu 7 Jan → Fitting 41 pieces of work into your free time → 128 checks passed.
- Strategy: 8 stages, each with a lesson, practice and a milestone check.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 100: No exams in view.; front_loading 45: 10 items finish on the due date with no room to run long.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Syllabus **Guitar from zero to a first full song** from model

Topics:

1. **Holding the guitar and first notes** w1 d1: Sit or stand with the guitar balanced and the neck at a comfortable angle; Tune all six strings with a tuner, then by matching fretted notes; Number strings and frets and find any note by name; Pluck single notes cleanly with a pick and with fingers; Press strings just behind the fret without muting neighbours
2. **Strumming and rhythm basics** w2 d1: Keep a steady down-strum at a slow tempo with a metronome; Strum from the wrist and elbow without tensing the shoulder; Count quarter and eighth notes out loud while strumming; Play down-up eighths evenly, brushing all strings; Mute unwanted strings to stop ringing between strums
3. **First open chords: Em, Am, E, A** w2 d1: Fret Em with two fingers and check every string rings; Play Am and A with clean, arched fingers; Play E major and hear its difference from Em; Change between Em and Am without stopping the strum; Use a chord pressure check: play each string one at a time
4. **Open chords: C, G, D, Dm** w3 d2: Play open C with the ring finger on the third fret of the A string; Play G with fingers placed to allow fast changes to C and D; Play D and Dm with the thin strings ringing clearly; Swap between C, G and D in slow, even changes; Anchor common fingers between chord pairs to reduce movement
5. **Changing chords in time** w3 d2: Change chords on the count without breaking the strum; Use one-minute changes to measure how many swaps you can make; Keep the strumming hand moving while the fretting hand moves; Simplify to down-strums on the beat, then add ups; Recover smoothly from a muffled or missed chord
6. **Common strumming patterns** w2 d2: Play the down-down-up-up-down-up eighth-note pattern; Play a pattern with a missed down-strum for a reggae feel; Accent the backbeat and keep the arm swinging; Count in 4/4 and feel bars and phrases; Loop a four-chord pattern without losing the beat
7. **Barre-free extras: capo, F alternatives** w1 d2: Use a capo to move easy chord shapes to a new key; Play Fmaj7 and F with a small barre as an alternative; Play a simple walk-up with bass notes between chords; Add a hammer-on and pull-off within a chord shape; Choose a key that fits your voice using the capo
8. **Playing your first full song** w3 d3: Read a chord chart with bars and repeat signs; Break the song into verse and chorus and learn each section; Play the whole song at a slow tempo with the metronome; Bring it up to full tempo with no stops; Play it through with the original recording; Keep going after mistakes and finish the song

## quiz-tomorrow: Chemistry quiz tomorrow on acids, bases and pH

*Edge case: a quiz tomorrow on a narrow topic.* Score **100**, planned as **exam**, due 2026-11-14, 29.5 s, 5 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning Chemistry quiz tomorrow on acids, bases and pH → Working out the plan → Looking up the syllabus → Read 6 sources → Double-checking the topics → Checking 3 more official topics → Double-check made 1 correction → 6 topics from the AI, checked against sources → Building your plan → 4 sessions to Fri 13 Nov → Fitting 4 pieces of work into your free time → 35 checks passed.
- Strategy: Quick revision of all 6 topics in 3 short sessions, then mixed practice on everything.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 75: new learning in the final day; front_loading 40: 4 items finish on the due date with no room to run long.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Syllabus **Acids, Bases and pH — class quiz** from model (researched)
- Papers: Quiz 40 min, 40 marks
- Sources: https://open.metu.edu.tr/bitstream/handle/11511/14334/index.pdf, http://hopatcongschools.org/files/do/curriculum/curricula/Advanced%20Placement%20Chemis.pdf, https://cbsd.org/site/handlers/filedownload.ashx?moduleinstanceid=61567&dataid=99936&FileName=Honors%20Review%20KEY.pdf, http://edu.gov.mb.ca/k12/cur/science/found/gr12_chem/unit5.pdf, https://app.oncoursesystems.com/curriculum/12792/32537848/unit/32754014, https://archive.org/download/PAKISTANITEXTBOOKS/1st%20Year%20Chemistry%20Textbook%20KP_text.pdf

Topics:

1. **Properties and definitions of acids and bases** w2 d2: List the characteristic properties of acids and bases, including taste, feel and litmus colour; Write the Arrhenius/Brønsted–Lowry definition of an acid and of a base; Identify the conjugate acid–base pairs in a given reaction; Classify species as Lewis acids or Lewis bases by electron-pair acceptance or donation; Explain how acid–base theories became broader from Arrhenius to Brønsted–Lowry to Lewis
2. **Strong and weak acids and bases** w2 d2: Distinguish strong from weak acids and bases using the degree of ionisation; Write equations showing full ionisation of strong acids and partial ionisation of weak acids; Explain how dilution and concentration affect pH for strong versus weak acids; Calculate percent ionisation from Ka or from concentrations; Use Ka, Kb and Kw to compare acid and base strength
3. **Water, Kw and pH calculations** w3 d2: Use Kw = [H+][OH-] = 1 × 10^-14 at 25 °C; Calculate pH from [H+] as -log[H+] and pOH from [OH-]; Convert between pH, pOH, [H+] and [OH-] using pH + pOH = 14; Work out [OH-] from a given pH or [H+] in an aqueous solution; Use the pH scale to classify a solution as acidic, neutral or basic; Use the heat of dissociation of water to explain why pH 7 is neutral only at 25 °C
4. **Determination of pH with probeware** w2 d2: Use a pH probe or pH meter to measure the pH of a range of solutions; Calibrate the probe with buffer solutions of known pH before measuring; Compare probeware readings with indicator or calculated pH values and discuss precision; Investigate how pH changes on dilution or on adding acid or base
5. **Neutralisation, salts and titration** w2 d2: Write balanced equations for acid plus base and acid plus carbonate reactions; Name the salt formed by a given acid–base reaction; Perform a titration to find the concentration of an unknown acid or base; Carry out titration calculations using moles and the reaction ratio; Predict whether a salt solution is acidic, basic or neutral from its parent acid and base
6. **Buffers and pH regulation** w1 d3: Describe how a buffer resists pH change on adding small amounts of acid or base; Write the equations showing a buffer reacting with added H+ and OH-; Identify a buffer system from its components, e.g. a weak acid with its salt

## typed-topics-future-start: CHEM 2410 Organic Chemistry I, whole module

*Edge case: the student types the topics and starts in ten days.* Score **100**, planned as **course**, due 2027-01-22, 30 s, 2 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning CHEM 2410 Organic Chemistry I, whole module → Mapping the course → Looking up the syllabus → Read 6 sources → 8 topics from your list, checked against sources → Building your plan → 42 sessions to Thu 21 Jan → Fitting 42 pieces of work into your free time → 68 checks passed.
- Strategy: 8 topics in order, 5 reviews, 1 checkpoint test.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 100: No exams in view.; front_loading 100: Deadline work finishes with room to spare.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 90 min
- Syllabus **Organic Chemistry I (CHEM 2410), whole module** from student (researched)
- Sources: https://mtu.edu/registrar/pdfs/undergraduate-course-descriptions-25-26.pdf, https://utu.ac.in/download/phd2018/Enterance%20Syllabus/Chemistry.pdf, https://chemistry.njit.edu/sites/chemistry/files/Chem%20243-2-ara.pdf, http://chem.uaf.edu/green/Chem321SyllabusFall2011.pdf, https://wizeprep.com/in-course-experience/Chem2410-saint-louis-university-main-campus/on-demand/chapter/5/checkpoint-quiz, https://moodle31.upei.ca/course/info.php?id=15326

Topics:

1. **Structure and bonding** w2 d2: Draw Lewis structures and assign formal charges; Use VSEPR to predict geometry and hybridisation of C, N and O; Describe bond polarity, dipoles and the shapes of molecules; Identify σ and π bonds and orbital overlap in bonding; Recognise acids and bases; rank them by pKa and predict acid–base equilibria; Draw resonance structures and assess their relative contributions; Predict the effect of inductive and resonance effects on electron density
2. **Alkanes and conformations** w2 d2: Name alkanes and branched alkanes by IUPAC rules; Draw constitutional isomers and identify the parent chain; Use Newman projections to analyse ethane and butane conformations; Rank staggered and eclipsed conformers by torsional and steric strain; Draw cyclohexane chair conformers and perform ring flips; Predict axial/equatorial preferences from 1,3-diaxial interactions; Write and balance combustion and halogenation reactions of alkanes; Explain the free-radical halogenation mechanism including propagation and termination
3. **Stereochemistry** w3 d3: Assign R/S configuration at stereocentres; Identify molecules with one and two stereocentres; Distinguish enantiomers, diastereomers and meso compounds; Determine whether a molecule is chiral or achiral by symmetry; Calculate specific rotation and use optical activity data; Use Fischer projections correctly, including rotation rules; Separate enantiomers by resolution and use enantiomeric excess calculations; Predict outcomes of stereospecific reactions (e.g. anti addition, inversion)
4. **Nucleophilic substitution** w3 d3: Classify substrates as methyl, primary, secondary or tertiary; Write SN2 mechanisms with complete arrow pushing and inversion; Write SN1 mechanisms via carbocation intermediates and racemisation; Rank leaving groups and nucleophiles by strength; Predict products from solvent, nucleophile, substrate and temperature; Describe carbocation stability and rearrangement in SN1; Compare and choose between SN1 and SN2 for a given reaction
5. **Elimination reactions** w3 d3: Write E2 mechanisms and draw the anti-periplanar transition state; Write E1 mechanisms via carbocations; Apply Zaitsev's rule and identify Hofmann products; Determine stereochemistry of E2 products (E/Z); Predict competition between substitution and elimination conditions; Choose base strength, size and temperature to favour elimination; Draw complete mechanisms for dehydrohalogenation and dehydration
6. **Alkenes and alkynes** w3 d3: Name alkenes and alkynes by IUPAC rules including E/Z; Describe stability of alkenes and heats of hydrogenation; Predict Markovnikov and anti-Markovnikov products of HX addition; Write mechanisms for hydration, hydroboration–oxidation and halogenation; Draw stereochemistry of syn and anti additions; Carry out oxidative cleavage (ozonolysis, KMnO4) and predict products; Write alkyne additions, including hydration and reduction to cis/trans alkenes; Use alkyne acidity in alkylation reactions
7. **Alcohols and ethers** w2 d2: Name and classify alcohols and ethers; describe hydrogen bonding and boiling points; Predict products of alcohol dehydration to alkenes; Convert alcohols to alkyl halides with HX, PBr3, SOCl2 and tosylates; Oxidise primary, secondary and tertiary alcohols with the right reagents; Prepare ethers by Williamson synthesis and explain why it needs a primary substrate; Cleave ethers with HI and identify products; Protect and deprotect alcohols with silyl ethers
8. **Spectroscopy** w3 d3: Use IR to identify functional groups from characteristic absorption bands; Assign 1H NMR chemical shifts to common proton environments; Use integration and splitting (n+1 rule) to determine structure; Interpret coupling constants to distinguish cis/trans and neighbouring protons; Use 13C NMR to count distinct carbon environments; Interpret mass spectra: molecular ion and common fragmentation patterns; Combine IR, NMR and MS data to propose a structure

## auto-vague-probability: I keep failing probability questions, especially conditional probability and Bayes

*Auto with a vague, personal request.* Score **100**, planned as **mastery**, due 2026-11-23, 7 s, 2 call(s). Stages: Reading your week → Reading your week · 0 commitments → Planning I keep failing probability questions, especially conditional → Working out the plan → 6 topics from the AI → Building your plan → 8 sessions to Sat 21 Nov → Fitting 8 pieces of work into your free time → 78 checks passed.
- Strategy: 6 parts, 1 recall check at widening gaps, then a mastery test. Time is tight, so sessions are short.
- Critics: realism 100: Every day is within what you usually sustain.; exam_prep 100: No exams in view.; front_loading 78: 3 items finish on the due date with no room to run long.; interleaving 100: Practice is mixed across subjects.; fragmentation 100: Sessions are a workable length.; neglect 100: Every active goal has time.
- Daily cap: 60 min
- Syllabus **Probability** from model

Topics:

1. **Sample spaces and events** w1 d1: List outcomes of experiments and count equally likely ones; Use Venn diagrams and set notation for unions, intersections, complements; Apply the addition rule P(A∪B) = P(A) + P(B) − P(A∩B); Identify mutually exclusive events and use P(A∩B) = 0; Use complementary events: P(A') = 1 − P(A)
2. **Counting methods for probability** w1 d2: Use the multiplication principle for successive choices; Count arrangements with permutations and selections with combinations; Handle repeat items and restricted positions; Use tree diagrams and outcome grids to count without error
3. **Independent events and tree diagrams** w2 d2: Apply P(A∩B) = P(A)P(B) for independent events; Test whether events are independent from given probabilities; Use tree diagrams for outcomes in sequence, with and without replacement; Complete a two-way table of probabilities and read joint probabilities
4. **Conditional probability** (weak) w3 d3: Write conditional probability in notation P(A|B) and interpret B as the new sample space; Apply P(A|B) = P(A∩B)/P(B) using counts and probabilities; Find conditional probabilities from two-way tables and Venn diagrams; Use tree diagrams to find conditional probabilities along branches; Use the multiplication rule P(A∩B) = P(B)P(A|B) to find joint probabilities; Recognise when P(A|B) ≠ P(B|A) and avoid swapping them; Solve multi-stage problems with given conditions on earlier outcomes
5. **Law of total probability and Bayes' theorem** w3 d3: Split an event by a partition and sum conditional cases; Apply P(A) = P(A|B)P(B) + P(A|B')P(B'); Use Bayes' theorem to reverse a conditional probability; Update probabilities with new information in tree and table problems; Interpret answers like false-positive rates and screening tests correctly
6. **Binomial and discrete distributions** w2 d2: Recognise fixed-trial binomial situations with constant probability; Compute binomial probabilities using C(n,r) p^r (1−p)^(n−r); Find mean and variance of a binomial: np and np(1−p); Use a given probability distribution table and its expectation
